It established that the Commission's reach now ran to the rank below the Directory and no higher; that it reached that rank only on documentary evidence and with the American lever behind it; and that a general who stole from his troops' rations could go to prison. The American press reported it as a sign that the Saigon government was cleaning itself up, and for a few weeks the corruption stories of the spring were about a conviction rather than a scandal. The officers of the IV Corps staff who replaced the convicted were chosen in part from the Commission's own list of officers with clean records, and there were not enough of them².

#### The night of the relief at My Phuoc Tay
*9–10 Mar 1967 · The PF post at Ap My Loi and the canal road to Cai Lay district town, My Phuoc Tay village, Cai Lay District, Dinh Tuong Province · new · EV008-4*
*Initiative: enemy*

This is the night the radio's second-order effect became visible, and the night that shows it best. By 1967 every Popular Forces post in the held belt of Dinh Tuong had a set on the district net, a PRC-25 from the Go Vap plant since the previous autumn, and a fresh battery every Thursday from the exchange at the district¹⁴. The Front had stopped assaulting those posts in 1965, as it had stopped assaulting the rearmed ones. In the first months of 1967 the Dinh Tuong provincial unit decided to use the radio against the men it called.

At 2235 on 9 March a mortar section of the 514th Local Force Battalion put twelve 60mm rounds into the post at Ap My Loi, a mud-walled fort on the canal six kilometres north of Cai Lay, from about 300 metres across the paddy. Sergeant Le Van Muoi, who commanded its PF platoon, called the district at 2237, reported the mortar and an assault forming on the dike to the west, and asked for the reaction force. The assault did not come. Two companies of the 514th were not on the dike at all; they were lying in the sugar cane on both sides of the canal road, three kilometres south of the post, where the road crossed a concrete culvert that the district's trucks had to slow to cross¹⁴.

The district chief, Major Nguyen Thanh Liem, did what the rule of 1964 told him, that a post that called would have its reaction within the hour, and sent the 247th Regional Force Company north in four trucks at 2250. At 2304 a command-detonated mine under the culvert lifted the lead truck off the road. The firing circuit, the district's engineers found in the morning, was two kilometres of WD-1 field wire from the Republic's own mill and a pack of Go Vap dry cells of the kind the post had been issued the week before. The two companies fired into the other three trucks from both sides for about eight minutes, took the weapons from the first, and were gone into the cane before the 7th Division's battery at Cai Lay had been given a target. The 247th lost 31 dead and 18 wounded and about 40 weapons, most of them M4A1 carbines and two M249s. The post itself lost one man, wounded by the mortar. The district reported 46 of the enemy killed; eight bodies were found at the culvert in the morning, and I believe the 514th lost between ten and fifteen killed¹⁴.

Liem wrote new rules the following week, and they are the reason the night belongs in the history. A post's first call would be for fire, not men: every post in the district had its concentrations plotted for the 105mm battery at Cai Lay and the 7th Division's guns at Cai Be, and the call was answered by the guns within four minutes. A relief would move by water or at first light, never by road at night on the one road the Front could predict. And a post that could hold behind its wire would be expected to. The rules spread through IV Corps in the spring, carried by the province advisers and by the 7th Division's artillery officers. In the months that followed, attacks on posts within range of the guns in Dinh Tuong fell by about a third, and the Front's district companies turned to the posts beyond the guns' 11 kilometres and to the posts whose batteries were run down at the end of the exchange week¹⁴.

The rules had a cost that the returns did not show and Liem's adviser did. A platoon that could call down the guns had less reason to leave its wire after dark, and in 1967 the night patrols of the PF platoons in Cai Lay fell by about a third; the post held itself, and the hamlet outside it was walked at night, more often than before, by the Front's tax collectors¹⁴. Ap My Loi held every attack made on it for the rest of the war. Whether the hamlet that bore its name was the government's after dark is a question the post's radio log cannot answer.

Sergeant Muoi had passed the leader's course at Nha Trang in 1961 and the platoon leader's course at the Dinh Tuong centre in 1964, and he kept a listening post of two men on the canal bank 300 metres south of the post every night. At 2241 they reported men moving south in the cane on the far side of the canal, toward the road, and Muoi added it to his report: the mortar, an assault forming to the west, and movement south toward the culvert. The district operations room logged it as the third of three lines. Liem read the first two and sent the trucks, because the rule said the reaction within the hour and the post had called; the sergeant had seen the ambush and the decision was not his⁶. The ambush went as told. The third of Liem's new rules came from Muoi's line in the log: a post's report of movement on the relief route stops the relief until the route is fired on or cleared at first light, and the district's posts were taught, through their platoon leaders, to report the road as well as the wire.

#### The Hill Fights at Khe Sanh
*24 Apr – 11 May 1967 · Hills 861, 881 South and 881 North, northwest of Khe Sanh, Quang Tri Province · no longer happens · EV000-34*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### CORDS is established
*9 May 1967 · Saigon · changed from history · EV000-35*
*Initiative: allied*
On 9 May 1967 the President placed the American pacification effort, civil and military, under MACV as Civil Operations and Revolutionary Development Support, with Robert W. Komer as one of Westmoreland's deputies, holding ambassadorial rank and authority over military personnel and resources, the first civilian to serve in such a role inside a US military command. It followed the failure of the Office of Civil Operations, set up in November 1966 to coordinate the civil agencies under civilian leadership. CORDS was about 85 percent military. Each corps had a CORDS deputy, usually civilian, under the corps senior adviser, and each province an American province senior adviser with civilian and military staffs alongside the Vietnamese province chief, usually an ARVN colonel⁷⁸.

Komer's priorities were the territorial forces and the attack on the VC infrastructure. The Regional and Popular Forces numbered about 300,000 in 1967; their advisory presence was later multiplied through Mobile Advisory Teams⁷⁸. In mid-1967 CORDS created the Intelligence Coordination and Exploitation programme, ICEX, to target the VCI, which became the Phoenix programme at the end of the year. The Hamlet Evaluation System, introduced in January 1967, began rating every hamlet monthly on a scale reported by the American district advisers; its figures were at once an indispensable record and a measure produced by officers rated on the result. CORDS solved the American management problem of pacification. It did not solve the Vietnamese one, that security in the hamlet at night depended on Vietnamese forces and officials who had to stay there.

In this war CORDS is established on the historical date and in the historical form; the provisions did not create a single manager. What CORDS inherits is different. The American land advisers, about 260 in 1964 and about 340 by 1967, pass into it as a land reform division under the CORDS deputy, and the provincial land services' register of titled parcels, which by May 1967 lists about 740,000 hectares and the households that hold them, becomes the first hamlet-by-hamlet household record the American pacification effort has ever had⁸. Komer adds titled hectares as a factor in the Hamlet Evaluation System's economic rating from its first revision. This headquarters objected in writing on 22 June 1967 that a title measured the government's paperwork and not the peasant's allegiance, that the Front had told its cadres to let peasants take titles and keep paying the Front's tax, and that titles issued would rise fastest where they meant least. The objection was recorded and the factor kept, at half the weight Komer had proposed. The Phung Hoang centres, which had used the land register since 1964 to locate the families of known cadres, passed into CORDS's reporting with it; the Commission had been receiving complaints about that use since 1965²¹¹.

In this war CORDS inherits a police as well as a land register. The aid mission's Public Safety Division, about 300 advisers in 1966, passes into CORDS as its Public Safety Directorate, and with it a National Police of about 70,000, about 1,500 village posts, and a Special Branch with district sections in about 200 districts and files on the infrastructure kept since 1964⁹. There is no ICEX in this war, because there is nothing for it to create: the Phung Hoang centres have sat in those district sections since 1964, under the district chief, with the Military Security Service and the sector S2 in the room, and every district has one by the end of 1965. The Provincial Reconnaissance Units were raised under the police statute in 1964 and answer to the province chiefs, not to the Agency; their methods are inside the statute's safeguards in law, and Duc Thanh in February showed how far that law reached in a village the government did not hold at night. Komer's contribution was the money to put a centre's American adviser in every contested district, which Hieu accepted, and a proposal for monthly targets, which the decree forbade¹¹. What the American pacification effort created in 1967 on the historical course, here it found already running, and the province senior advisers found a counterpart chief who had been in his post for two or three years³.

#### Operation Buffalo and the siege of Con Thien
*2 Jul – 31 Oct 1967 · Con Thien and "the Marketplace", Quang Tri Province, south of the DMZ · no longer happens · EV000-36*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### Hanoi decides on a general offensive
*6 Jul 1967 – Jan 1968 · Hanoi and COSVN · changed from history · EV000-37*
In April 1967 COSVN under General Nguyen Chi Thanh proposed a decisive general offensive in the South, and in May sent the plan to Hanoi. Thanh briefed the Politburo in July and died there on 6 July, by the most common account of a heart attack after a party, by another of wounds from a US bombing raid. The plan was carried forward by the militants around Le Duan and Le Duc Tho; General Vo Nguyen Giap, who had favoured protracted war, did not originate it and modified it reluctantly. Beginning on 27 July, hundreds of moderates, officers and intellectuals were arrested in the "Revisionist Anti-Party Affair", with a second wave in November and December, which removed opposition to the offensive and much of Giap's staff⁷⁹.

The premise was that the ARVN was no longer effective and the Saigon government unpopular, and that a general offensive in the cities would set off a general uprising. In October the Politburo chose the Tet holiday of 1968 as the date; it reaffirmed the decision in December and formalized it as Resolution 14 in January 1968⁷⁹. The border battles of the autumn, at Con Thien, Loc Ninh, Song Be and Dak To, were part of the preparation, drawing US forces away from the cities⁷⁰. This was a decision taken in Hanoi for Hanoi's reasons: the stalemate in the South, the factional struggle in the Party and a reading of the South that proved wrong.

In this war the decision is taken on the same dates by the same men, and for the same reasons; the provisions did not make Le Duan a militant or remove Giap's staff. They added an argument to the militants' case and subtracted one from its premise. COSVN's reports of 1966–67 recorded that the Front's agricultural tax in the Delta was falling, that recruitment in the titled districts along the Bassac was harder each season, and that the "land trick" had not been exposed as the cadres had promised; Thanh's plan cited the erosion of the base as a reason not to wait. At the same time the premise that the Saigon government was unpopular in the countryside was weaker in the Delta than anywhere else, and COSVN's own appreciation for Military Region 9 in the autumn said so; it was overruled². The urgency rose and the forecast of a general uprising was carried forward unchanged, so that in this war the offensive was planned on a reading of the South that was more wrong in the Delta than the historical one.

The premise about the ARVN was wrong in a second way here, and the purge made sure nobody in Hanoi said so. In the spring of 1967 a study group of the General Staff's combat research department, working for Giap from the B2 and B3 Fronts' after-action reports since 1964, had concluded that the puppet infantry and its territorials, "re-equipped from the first year with American automatic weapons of a new type", no longer broke in close combat as they had in 1963 and that the local forces could not expect to overrun district towns defended by them without main-force support. The group's three senior officers were among those arrested in the second wave in December, for reasons that had nothing to do with their study and everything to do with whose staff they were on, and the study went into the files with them²¹. The offensive was planned, as historically, on the assumption that the ARVN would dissolve on contact. In this war it had been better armed than its attackers for four years.

#### The Phu Cat lot
*14 Aug – 20 Dec 1967 · A firing position of the 22nd Division's artillery on Route 1 south of Phu Cat, Binh Dinh Province; the Bien Hoa arsenal; the Special Court for Corruption, Saigon · new · EV008-5*
*Initiative: neither*

On the morning of 14 August 1967 an M101A1 105mm howitzer of a 22nd Division battery firing in support of a sweep of the Phu Cat hills burst its tube on the eleventh round of a mission. Four of the crew were killed and three wounded. The round was high explosive from a lot the Bien Hoa arsenal had forged and loaded in May, and within a week the 22nd Division's artillery had found two more rounds of the same lot with cracks at the base of the body that a gauge would have caught¹⁴.

The investigation was the Commission's, and it was quick because the paper was good. Truong Vinh Khoa's inspectors found that the lot had been accepted by a Directorate inspector on the strength of the forging line's own records, without the proof-firing of a sample that the specification required; the inspector had accepted four lots that way since March, and had been paid for the time he saved the line. He was convicted by the Special Court in December and sentenced to six years. The arsenal recalled about 40,000 rounds from five lots and destroyed about 9,000 of them¹⁴.

The gunners' verdict took longer to reverse than the court's. Through the autumn of 1967 and into 1968 the artillery of II Corps, and much of the rest of the army's, refused domestic 105mm rounds where it could draw American, and the share of 105mm bodies bought under the Aid Compliance Board's rule fell from about a third to about a tenth for most of a year. What restored it was the officers the 1964 courses had made: the Ordnance Directorate's acceptance branch, under captains and majors who could read a metallurgical report, rewrote the procedure so that every lot was proof-fired at the Long Thanh range with an officer's signature on the result, and the 22nd Division's own artillery commander was invited to witness the first ten¹⁴. Binh Dinh's gunners went back to Bien Hoa's rounds in the summer of 1968. It was a small event, and the reason it is in the record is that it showed, three years before the palace took the Directorate, what an arsenal is worth without honest acceptance: less than nothing, because a soldier who does not trust his round will not fire it.

#### The constitution and the presidential election
*3 Sep 1967 · Republic of Vietnam · historical · EV000-38*
The Constituent Assembly elected in September 1966 produced a constitution, promulgated on 1 April 1967. In the presidential election of 3 September 1967, held with the Senate election, Thieu ran for president with Ky as his running mate, after the generals forced Ky to stand aside. Eleven tickets ran. Thieu and Ky won 34.83 percent, 1,649,561 votes; the runner-up, Truong Dinh Dzu, a lawyer campaigning on negotiations with the NLF, won 17.26 percent, 817,120 votes. The reported turnout was 83.17 percent of 5,853,251 registered voters. There were credible reports of soldiers showing voters how to mark their ballots, and of a secret military committee that would govern behind the elected government; on 10 August 57 members of the US House had condemned the conduct of the campaign. Dzu was later imprisoned. Thieu was inaugurated on 31 October and the lower house was elected on 22 October⁸⁰.

The election gave the Republic of Vietnam an elected government for the first time since 1963 and, under Thieu, the stability that the previous four years had lacked. It did not give it legitimacy in the countryside: two-thirds of the voters chose someone else, and a civilian peace candidate had come second.

#### ◆ The empty beds of Dinh Tuong
*4 Sep – 14 Nov 1967 · Cai Lay, Cai Be and Giao Duc Districts, Dinh Tuong Province; Memot, Kampong Cham Province, Cambodia · new · EV014-5*
*Initiative: both*

This is the operation that taught the command what the unit had actually done, and it did so by failing for ten weeks.

In the first week of September 1967 the National Fusion Centre gave the Can Tho squadron twenty-six addresses in the three upper districts of Dinh Tuong: the province committee's five surviving members, the finance section's chief, and twenty of the collectors and couriers who had been named in the ledgers taken in 1966. The file was the best in the Delta. The province had a post in every village the government held, a resident constable in most of them, a family census three years deep and a land register that said who tilled what. Between 4 September and 14 November the squadron mounted twenty-three raids on those addresses, most of them inside two hours of the decision and all at night.

It took two. A courier of nineteen was lifted out of a house in Cai Be on 19 September with a bag of receipts, and a village committee secretary at Giao Duc on 2 October. The other twenty-one raids found a bed that had been slept in and a family that said, truthfully, that the man had not been there for months.

The courier told them why, and the task force's interrogators had it out of her in two days. The province committee had not lived in Dinh Tuong since March. It lived at Memot, in the rubber across the Cambodian border, with the region's finance section, and it came into the province for four or five nights every second month, moving every night, sleeping in a different hamlet each time and never in a house named in a ledger. The rule had come down in a COSVN circular in April 1967, over Pham Hung's name — he had taken the Central Office when Nguyen Chi Thanh died in Hanoi in July, and the circular was Thanh's last instruction carried out by his successor⁸¹. It had three sentences in it: senior cadre do not sleep twice in a hamlet; committees meet in the base areas or across the border and never in villages; couriers carry nothing written.

At Tan Son Nhut in November the argument ran the way arguments about metrics run. The unit's raid yield in the Delta had fallen by about two-fifths from 1966 and the natural reading was that the squadron had gone slack. Vance's J-2 and the task force's analysts read it the other way and were right: the committees of the upper Delta had been driven out of the province they administered, were running it by courier from across an international border at two months' remove, and the falling yield was the measure of that success and not of failure. The Front paid for it in the only currency it had to pay in. The province's collections from the posted villages ran at about a sixth of 1964's through 1967, its rice procurement in the three districts fell with them, and the committee that had once set a village's tax rate in person now learned of a bad harvest six weeks after it happened³¹. It did not pay in people. The adaptation cost the Front a courier's walking time, which against 1,310 Americans and the best three hundred operators in their army is the cheapest answer any provision in this record has drawn from the enemy.

The command changed its measure in December 1967. Delta's reporting after that gave the raid count, and beside it the Front's collections in the raided districts and the distance the committees lived from them.

#### The barrier and Muscle Shoals
*8 Sep – Dec 1967 · The DMZ and the Laotian panhandle; Nakhon Phanom, Thailand · no longer happens · EV000-39*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-4; the event still happens.*

#### The battle of Dak To
*3–23 Nov 1967 · Dak To and Hills 1338, 724 and 875, Kontum Province · no longer happens · EV000-40*
*Consolidated at CP02 into EVCP02-3; the event still happens.*

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1967 the United States has about 486,000 military personnel in Vietnam and has lost 9,378 of them killed by hostile action in the year¹. Rolling Thunder has dropped about 864,000 tons on North Vietnam by the Pentagon's announcement of 31 December⁴³, and the North's air defences have made every strike near Hanoi expensive. The Trail is carrying more than ever; truck movements rise sharply from October to December. The enemy order of battle is in dispute: MACV counts about 300,000 communist personnel, the CIA up to 430,000⁷⁹, and the disagreement turns on whether the guerrillas and the political infrastructure, the part of the enemy the main-force war does not touch, are counted at all.

On 21 November Westmoreland tells the National Press Club that the enemy is "unable to mount a major offensive" and that "the end begins to come into view"⁷⁹. The administration's progress campaign of the autumn rests on the body count, the Hamlet Evaluation System and the claim that the crossover point is near. McNamara, who has lost confidence in the war, has announced his departure for the World Bank, and Johnson's offer at San Antonio on 29 September, to stop the bombing in exchange for prompt and productive talks, has been rejected by Hanoi. In Saigon there is, for the first time since 1963, an elected government under Thieu, and in the countryside CORDS has begun to organize the territorial war, from a less low base than history's. About 820,000 hectares have been titled to about 640,000 families, rent has ended in law and largely in fact wherever the government holds the night, and the Commission has convicted a general. The infrastructure of the Front is smaller in the Delta than on the historical course, and its tax take lower, but it is intact in every hamlet the government does not hold after dark, and those hamlets are still most of the contested countryside².

In Hanoi the decision is taken. The PAVN and VC main forces have been drawn to the borders and have drawn the Americans after them; the VC's local forces and cadres in and around the cities are preparing; and the Politburo has set the Tet holiday of 1968 as the moment for the General Offensive and General Uprising. The phase ends with each side certain that the other is weaker than it is, and with the population of the South, the object of the war, still largely outside the reach of either army's strategy.

In the held belt the resident infrastructure has been driven out of most villages with settled posts by the end of 1967, and the arrest count has fallen because there is little left there to arrest³. The National Police numbers about 80,000, the chiefs have mostly held their districts for two or three years, and the Front's legal cadres are in the towns, where the police knows less.

The enemy's external supply is measured: the Trail by the panhandle watch, Sihanoukville by the count, the North's roads by the Gulf watch, and every measure says that what arrives exceeds what the South's forces need from the North¹⁹. That knowledge is honest from 1965 rather than from 1970, and it has not changed the rules, the bombing or the strategy. Its main effect on Washington has been to deliver bad news early.

The RVNAF enters 1968 with the rifles it has carried for four years and a Regional Force that the corps commanders want for their own operations, bound since 1967 by the rule that keeps it in its provinces. The American army has had no rifle crisis and no inquiry, and has lost somewhat fewer men in the first minutes of its fights; it is the same army in every other respect, fighting the same battles on the enemy's terms.

The bombers have learned two things in two years: that a bridge they can measure dies in a night and is replaced by five crossings they cannot, and that a siege they can measure becomes the enemy's worst battle. The second lesson is the one the headquarters remembers going into 1968.

Phung Hoang has a centre in every district and an audited count of about 18,000 neutralized since 1965, about one in five of them members of district or village committees, and the Front's district administration in the held belt now governs from the contested edge by courier. The word Phoenix has been a press story since March 1967. About 7,000 selected advisers hold every field post below corps; the American battalions have changed over once or twice each as units, have fought better in the contacts they were granted, and have learned at Dien Ban to keep their reliefs off the dark of the moon. The staggered six-week overlap that followed raised the peak American strength in theater by about 15,000–25,000 men, which the expansion battalions of 1967 waited for, so that the enemy's tactic against the relief was paid for in American spaces rather than American dead¹¹. None of it has changed who chooses the battle.

The ARVN ends 1967 with about four in five of its battalions commanded by officers the board chose, on eighteen-month commands, with the captain-led battalion nearly gone and the thirty-day review feared and read; about a third fewer of its battalions have been rendered ineffective in 1966–67 than on the historical course, which no report shows because the enemy declined the fights that would have shown it¹². The 25th Division has a commander from the list and the corps have the commanders they had. The Military Security Service has its first file on the board's officers as a political factor, and COSVN has had every list the board published.

The factories end 1967 producing what the domestic share buys, with the steelworks short of power and the arsenal short of its gunners' trust after Phu Cat. Every post of the held belt has a radio, the Front attacks those that can call about a third less often than those that cannot, and where the guns can reach the posts the first call is for fire¹⁴. The posts hold themselves better than the hamlets around them.

The ARVN ends 1967 trained to the man; training alone would have left it as short of squad leaders as history's, and what filled most of the empty slots was the NCO schooling described below; its battalions have lost about a tenth to a sixth fewer men in the ambushes the enemy still lays, beyond what the rifles and the officers saved, and about one in eight fewer battalions has been broken in 1966–67 than the officer reforms alone would have left. The drill-sergeant corps has begun to return about 650–1,800 platoon sergeants a year to the battalions¹⁵. In the lowlands of Quang Nam and Quang Ngai the mine has replaced the firefight as the enemy's way of killing Americans, and in the districts around Saigon the Front's guerrilla squads are drilled by men the Republic trained.

The ARVN ends 1967 with its squads and platoons led by schooled men: about two in three of the slots the field commissions emptied are held by corporals first class who passed the leader's course, and the squads of the 7th Division held their base of fire at Hau My when their officers fell. I judge that about one in six to one in five fewer ARVN battalions were broken in 1966–67 than the officer reforms and the basic course alone would have left, and that the posts of the Delta lost about a tenth to a fifth fewer to night attack than the radio and the trained platoon had already saved⁶. None of it shows in a return: the enemy attacked less what kept its routine. American sergeants pass the Basic Leader Course at Bien Hoa or wait for it, and the waiver of 1966 has become a ninety-day deferral.

The combined command ends 1967 with one corps area under a Vietnamese commander and three under American deputies who in practice run them, a quarterly plan the corps commanders execute at their own speed, and a two-layer plan that protects its operations and insults its Vietnamese staff. The task force has broken the Front's district government of the Saigon belt from its edge: the Hoc Mon committee and the region's finance section govern from the Ho Bo woods, and the region's collections from the posted villages are about an eighth of 1964's¹⁶. On the II Corps coast it has a list it abandoned and a story the Front still tells.

Over the North the phase ends with the campaign the theater asked for and the result it predicted. In October 1966 this command reported that the northern campaign was destroying everything in the North that could be destroyed and that the North's war in the South needed none of it, and the barrier began from that report. The air defence that history's gradualism let the North build under sanctuary was built instead under fire and from China: about 120 MiGs fighting from Chinese fields, and SA-2 battalions three sites to a launcher. About 40–90 fewer American aircraft were lost over the North in 1966–67 than historically, and the Stennis hearings on the restrictions were never held⁷.

Delta ends the phase with its rate found and its limit found with it. Thirty-eight raids a month in 1966 and 44 in 1967, 297 listed cadre at district level or above in the two years, 61 of them province or region committee members, and nine finance sections taken with their ledgers; four in five of the raids in III and IV Corps, because that is where the file is. Ninety-four reconnaissance missions into the Laotian panhandle under the ambassador's limits produced the first measured coordinates under canopy in this war and changed what arrives in the South by one to four points, inside the width the phase already holds³¹. Fourteen operators are dead, four missing near the Ban Laboy ford, fifty-one wounded, and about a quarter of the men in the assault troops were selected after the war began, because the unit was committed whole and has nowhere to rotate to. And in the upper Delta the enemy has answered the whole provision with three sentences in a COSVN circular: senior cadre do not sleep twice in a hamlet, committees meet across the border, couriers carry nothing written. The committees of Dinh Tuong now govern their districts from Memot at two months' remove, their collections at about a sixth of 1964's, and the raid yield that measures this success is falling.

### Mechanism

This is the provision's phase and it produces three things, in descending order of weight.

The first is the senior cadre taken: 297 at district level or above in thirty months, 61 of them province or region committee members, nine finance sections with their ledgers, concentrated four to one in III and IV Corps because that is where the file is. Compounded with the task force this accelerates the destruction of the Front's upper administration in the held belt, and the end vector carries it as 40–150 a year inside the existing width of FIND.vci rather than as a new band, because that is its true size against a file that was already taking 300–700 infrastructure members a month (M012).

The second is the first measured coordinates under canopy in this war. The record's limit on precision has been location and not accuracy since memorandum 005, and under the trees there was no instrument at all; ninety-four missions into the panhandle produced 134 measured points, a third of them under canopy. Its effect on what arrives in the South is one to four points, inside the width the phase holds, because Group 559's answer to precision was to stop owning anything measurable and that answer was given in 1966. I judge FIND.main_force in units for the first time on this evidence, at 0–3 points, and the low end is the honest reading.

The third is the ten weeks in Dinh Tuong, which is the event this memorandum narrates at length because it embodies the second-order analysis rather than the provision's primary use. Twenty-three raids, two men, twenty-one slept-in beds, and the reason in a captured courier's statement: the committees had gone to Memot. The command changed its measure in December 1967 from the raid count to the Front's collections and the distance the committees lived from their districts, which is the record's own standing rule about metrics applied in time rather than after the fact.

Under DIRMT01-11 the rice and the piastre: in the three upper districts of Dinh Tuong the Front's collections ran at about a sixth of 1964's through 1967 and its rice procurement fell with them, because a committee that sets a village's assessment in person and a committee that learns of the harvest six weeks late are not the same instrument³¹. Nothing in this provision touches the soldier's pay or the Saigon price index, and the devaluation of June 1966 cut the private's rice by a third regardless.

### Enemy response and initiative

Detection took about eighteen months and the conclusion was correct. By early 1967 COSVN had the pattern: men taken from houses at night, no cordon, no sweep, nobody seen, helicopters heard afterwards. Pham Hung's circular of April 1967, carrying out the last instruction of Nguyen Chi Thanh, who died in Hanoi in July, contained three sentences and travelled from the Central Office to the district cells in a season — and the enemy got two further things right. He concluded that the night-raiders could not cross the border, because in three years they never did, which made Cambodia the specific answer to this specific threat; and he concluded from the concentration of the raids that the unit was small, which made dispersal rather than fortification the right response.

What he got wrong was its nature, and the error helped him. The Front described Delta as an American assassination unit, as it had described Phoenix historically and the task force in this record, and here the description was close enough to true — soldiers entering houses at night without warrants outside the held belt — that it worked in the coastal lowlands where the raids were least accurate.

The adaptation's cost is the judgment of the phase. In the base areas the enemy did pay something: standing security on the trail junctions from 1966, listening posts, movement without lights, and the eighteen-month decay of the teams' advantage that follows. In the hamlets he paid a courier's walking time.

### Causal chains

**C014-2** [war-shaping] (begins in Phase II) Delta's raids take 61 province and region committee members out of the Saigon belt and the upper Delta in 1966–67, on the task force's files → COSVN issues the three-sentence rule in April 1967 and the committees of the upper Delta move to Memot, inside Cambodia, visiting their districts four or five nights every second month → the Front's district administration in those provinces is run by courier at two months' remove, its collections fall to about a sixth of 1964's, and its senior cadre are therefore outside the reach of every allied instrument including this one, so that in 1969–71 the national file names men that nobody can touch (EV014-7, P4 end vector) → when Lon Nol's coup and the incursion of May 1970 push the Cambodian sanctuary north to Kratie, the Front's southern administration moves with it and is further from the Delta in 1971 than it has ever been, which is why the countryside of 1969–71 is the best the government has held and why none of it is a measure of the enemy's organisation being beaten.

The terminal link is not predictable from the first: a raid force applied to the senior apparatus ends by making the government's pacification figures better and the enemy's apparatus safer in the same movement.

**C014-3** [months] (begins in Phase II) Delta's teams go into the Laotian panhandle under the ambassador's limits from October 1965 and bring back 134 measured points, a third under canopy → the coordinate cell at Tan Son Nhut can put a GBU-31 on a truck park under trees for the first time, and does, through 1966–67 → Group 559's answer is the answer it gave to HARD CROSSING, which is to disperse what can be measured, so the parks fragment into a dozen dispersal sites each and the measured point loses its value within a season → the lasting product is not tonnage but the ledger: a team's count of what passed a junction on a given night is the only interdiction figure in this war that was never contested in Washington, and it is what makes the Commando Hunt claims of 1969–71 indefensible from the first week.

### Chain review

**C012-2**, written by memorandum 012 and terminating in an event this memorandum did not write, resolves differently now and in the enemy's favour. Its course was: STILL WATER drives the Hoc Mon committee out of its district → FOLLOW THROUGH reaches Cu Chi and takes the region finance section's ledger, and the committee and the section go to the Ho Bo woods → nobody quarters or guides the assault companies in the Ho Bo woods at Tet. With Delta raiding the base-area fringes from 1966, the Ho Bo woods is no longer far enough: the Hoc Mon committee and the region's finance section leave it for the Cambodian side of the Fishhook during 1967, as the Dinh Tuong committees left for Memot. The terminal link holds — nobody quarters the assault companies in January 1968 — and it holds harder, because the committee is now a border away rather than a day's walk. The cost is that the committee survives Tet intact where in memorandum 012's version it was within reach of a cross-province warrant. The chain's author predicted the outcome; he did not predict that a further provision would buy the same outcome at the price of putting the organisation permanently beyond arrest.

### Downstream

Phase III inherits a unit at its peak of experience and a measure the command has already corrected. Phase IV inherits an enemy apparatus whose senior layer is in Cambodia, which is the single most consequential downstream effect in this memorandum, and the folders the teams will build there from September 1967. The 81st Special Group, agreed in February 1968, is the only thing in the provision that outlives the American withdrawal.

## §4.P3 Phase III — Tet and its aftermath (Jan 1968 – Dec 1968)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: POP.night_control 79–84 (history 76.3; medium) · POP.night_control.I_corps 0 to +2 against history (medium) · POP.night_control.II_corps +1 to +4 against history (medium) · POP.night_control.III_corps +6 to +12 against history (medium) · POP.night_control.IV_corps +5 to +12 against history (medium) · POP.displacement -40 to 0 against history (low) · POP.gvn_legitimacy +2 to +6 against history (low) · POP.gvn_legitimacy.I_corps -1 to +1 against history (low) · POP.gvn_legitimacy.II_corps -2 to +1 against history (low) · POP.gvn_legitimacy.III_corps +2 to +6 against history (low) · POP.gvn_legitimacy.IV_corps +4 to +10 against history (low) · VCI.strength -45% to -30% against history (medium) · ENEMY.engagement_control 63–68 (history 68; low) · TRAIL.tonnage_arriving -17% to -5% against history (low) · FIND.main_force 0 to +4 against history (low) · FIND.vci +1.55 to +3.15 against history (medium) · ARVN.effectiveness -35% to -15% against history (low) · ARVN.effectiveness.small_unit +3 to +8 against history (low) · ARVN.self_sufficiency -3 to +2 against history (low) · ARVN.self_sufficiency.fire_support -5 to +2 against history (low) · US.casualties 14,450–14,560 (history 14,592; low) · US.political_tolerance.public 39–44 (history 41; low).*

1968 is the year the war turns, and it turns on a battle the enemy lost. In the first hours of 31 January PAVN and VC forces attack Saigon, Hue and most of the provincial capitals of the South at once, breaking the Tet truce and every assumption of the American progress campaign. By the measures of the battlefield the General Offensive fails: there is no general uprising, the cities are retaken, Hue after a month, and the Viet Cong lose the larger part of the cadres and local forces they had built over a decade. By the measures of politics it succeeds beyond Hanoi's hopes. Within two months the President has refused Westmoreland's request for 206,000 more men, offered negotiations, kept the bombing of the whole North, and withdrawn from the presidential race. US deaths by hostile action reach 14,592 in the year, the highest of the war, and US strength in Vietnam stands at about 537,000 at its end¹.

The phase has three offensives, in January–March, May–June and August–September, and three American responses. The first is military: Khe Sanh is held under the heaviest bombardment in the history of air warfare, the cities are cleared, and a new commander, General Creighton W. Abrams, takes over in June. The second is political: the decision of 31 March caps the American commitment, and in this war, with no partial halt to trade, the talks wait for the full halt in November. The third is the countryside: with the VC's main and local forces weakened and its infrastructure exposed in the offensive, the government and CORDS move into the vacuum, with a general mobilization in June, the Phoenix programme and an Accelerated Pacification Campaign from November. The year ends with the bombing of the North stopped, a new president elected on a promise to end the war, and the war's centre of gravity shifting from the American divisions to the territorial forces and the hamlets.

The human cost in the South is the heaviest of the war to date. The offensive and the counter-attacks create about 630,000 new refugees, on top of about 800,000 displaced before; more than 70,000 homes are destroyed; by the end of the year about one South Vietnamese in twelve lives in a refugee camp. The government counts 14,300 civilian dead and 24,000 wounded in the first phase alone⁷⁹.

In this war the year runs on the same dates and turns the same way. Tet is still decided in Saigon, Hue and Khe Sanh, and in Washington². What changes is the Delta, the hamlets and the army that met the offensive.

In the held belt the Front attacks from a smaller base. It recruits fewer men in the titled districts of the Bassac and the coastal Centre in 1968. The hamlets around the western Delta towns warn the garrisons and do not hide the attackers. The Special Branch knows the Front's urban cadres. Its guides do not arrive. A third of the leaders it sends into the Delta towns to direct the uprising are taken in February, because the district centres had charted them for three years²³¹¹. On the northwestern road to Tan Son Nhut its columns cross Hoc Mon through hamlets that have no resident cadres left to guide them. In Hue its lists are better than elsewhere, because the Republic's own census fed them. The province centre's informant cards burn in the first hour; the district centres' cards do not.

The warning was in the files and did not change the night. By January the task force's corps groups held caches, couriers and committees that pointed at the towns around Tet. The combined estimate read them as local, and the leave order stood⁸².

The ARVN and the territorials go into Tet changed in four ways:
- every man carries a modern rifle;
- every man has passed the basic course;
- battalion commanders were chosen by the board;
- sergeants have been schooled.

In the district towns about a third of the attacks never get inside the compound walls. In IV and III Corps the battalions recall their men faster. The wounded of both allied armies are alive in larger numbers in the morning. At Cai Lay the posts that kept their listening posts out see the 514th Battalion coming and are left alone²¹¹²¹⁵⁶. In the cities the PAVN and the main force fight block by block, and there all of this lowers the cost without shortening the battles. Hue, fought under one combined commander, ends five days early¹⁶. The sappers who attack the combined headquarters carry a plan a traitor had drawn, and the compound holds.

When the government and CORDS move into the countryside after the offensives, they move into hamlets where about 900,000 hectares are already titled. Phoenix is only the name Washington learned for district centres that had run since 1964 without quotas and under an audit, and the Commission audits it against the land register².

The mobilization of June finds rifles in stock, a training command that keeps the course at ten weeks, and platoon leaders who have passed the leader's course in about fourteen platoons in fifteen; its new units reach the field trained and a month late, and the academy and the sergeants' ranks pay for it¹²¹⁵⁶. The Front's third offensive goes for what no course reached, the People's Self-Defense Force, and COSVN works on the officers the board had passed over.

Above all this the radar watches the border. At Khe Sanh it measures the siege every night and its trenches are destroyed before they reach the wire; the PAVN pulls back about two weeks early at a much heavier price, and the garrison loses about a third fewer men. It is the most complete American victory of the war on the ground and changes nothing in Washington, and the brigade pulled from Thua Thien's hamlets to win it is missing when the Front returns to them. Abrams refuses to make it a doctrine of border bases and closes Khe Sanh on the historical date. After the halt the radar measures how the North uses it, and Commando Hunt opens against a panhandle net built to be cut, with a second ledger beside the Air Force's claims.

The President's decision of 31 March has no partial halt in it, because the campaign he had authorized in 1965 allowed none. Hanoi will not talk while the bombs fall, so the May and August offensives are fought with no table in Paris. The northern campaign goes on above the 19th parallel through the summer. It costs about 40 aircraft over the Hanoi and Haiphong gun belts, which history's campaign never risked⁷. The halt of 1 November comes on history's date and opens the talks themselves. The four-party conference meets in January, as it did historically: the six months lost in Paris were months history had spent on statements.

#### The siege of Khe Sanh
*21 Jan – 15 Apr 1968 · Khe Sanh Combat Base, Lang Vei and the surrounding hills, Quang Tri Province; Route 9 in Laos to Ban Houei Sane · changed from history · EV000-41*
*Initiative: enemy*

In January 1968 the PAVN massed around the Marine base at Khe Sanh, in the northwest corner of Quang Tri Province astride Route 9 to Laos. The 304th and 325C Divisions were close in, with the 320th and 324th Divisions in the region. The base and its hill outposts were held by the 26th Marines under Colonel David E. Lownds, with the 1st Battalion, 9th Marines and other battalions, and from 27 January the ARVN 37th Ranger Battalion⁸³.

In this war the concentration was watched from the day it began. From mid-December the panhandle watch tracked the 304th Division's vehicles coming down Route 9 out of Laos and the trucks of Group 559 turning east from Tchepone toward Ban Houei Sane, about 20–25 a night rising to about 80 by mid-January. The U-2S's signals payload fixed the headquarters of the 325C Division northwest of Hill 881 North on 2 January and that of the 304th near Co Roc, across the border, on 11 January, and from the volume and pattern of their traffic the J-2 analysts identified a front headquarters forming above them. It was the most complete picture of an enemy concentration this command had ever held. Westmoreland, who expected Hanoi to attempt "Dien Bien Phu in reverse", read it as confirmation, and after Con Thien he read it as an opportunity: here, he told his staff on 13 January, the enemy would give us the battle we could win⁸³. The radar plots went every morning to Saigon and every evening to the White House, where the President kept a terrain model of Khe Sanh in the Situation Room¹⁹. The 1st Cavalry Division moved north to I Corps in January, as it did historically, and in this war a brigade of the 101st Airborne Division moved with it, drawn from the coastal districts of Thua Thien where it had been working with the Regional Forces²³.

The picture showed the border and nothing else. The forces that would attack the cities on 30–31 January moved into them in small groups, on foot and by bus and sampan among the millions travelling home for Tet, under the eyes of their own people; nothing in the radar picture warned of Saigon, Hue or the provincial towns. General Frederick Weyand's decision of 10 January to pull fifteen battalions back toward Saigon rested, as it did historically, on signals and ground intelligence about the enemy's units in III Corps¹⁹. The radar deepened the American fixation on Khe Sanh, which history had without it; the siege box, by promising a victory there, deepened it again.

The siege began on 21 January with an assault on Hill 861 and a bombardment that blew up the base's main ammunition dump. The answer was Operation Niagara, and in this war Niagara had two halves. The first was the historical one: about 60 B-52s on an average day, sensors and reconnaissance, more than 158,000 artillery rounds in the first three months, and boxes on the valleys, the parks and Route 9 in Laos that the radar had seen fill during the night⁸³¹⁹. It fell as a carpet half as dense as the Big Belly's, because the J carries about half the D's unguided bombs, and in tonnage of unguided bombs Niagara delivered about two-thirds of its historical weight²⁴. The second half was new. From 23 January a Global Hawk stood over the Khe Sanh plateau every night, and its radar imaged the ground within three kilometres of the perimeter each hour; Sorrell had moved a detachment of the coordinate cell to Da Nang, and from the night of 26 January cells of three Js arrived every ninety minutes, each carrying thirty to forty GBU-38s aimed at positions measured that night²³.

What the PAVN had come to do at Khe Sanh, it did by digging. Historically the trenches of the 304th and 325C Divisions crept toward the perimeter from mid-February and reached within a few dozen metres of the wire on the east, where the 37th Rangers held⁸³. Here the trench lines were imaged as they were dug. A trench opened at dusk was on the cell's board before midnight and under a GBU-38 before dawn, and from 15 February the cells were cleared to bomb as close as 400 metres to the Marines and Rangers, inside the kilometre that history's carpets kept²⁴. The 304th Division dug three times toward the eastern perimeter, on 17, 22 and 27 February, and each time its trenches were destroyed at 500–800 metres. On the night of 29 February, when a regiment of the 304th formed to assault the Rangers' sector, it formed in the open beyond the trenches it had not been able to keep, and the GBU-38 cells and the Marine artillery broke it before it reached the wire, as they did historically, at a higher cost to the attackers. The PAVN's guns at Co Roc across the border, 130mm and 152mm pieces rolled out of caves to fire, were struck at their firing positions by GBU-31(V)3/Bs within hours of each mission, and by mid-March they fired from Co Roc only at night and only a few rounds a gun²³.

On the night of 6–7 February the panhandle watch tracked a column of eleven or twelve slow vehicles leaving the Ban Houei Sane area eastward on Route 9, and the warning reached the Special Forces camp at Lang Vei about forty minutes before twelve PT-76s of the 203rd Armored Regiment, with infantry of the 304th Division, attacked it at about 0030 on 7 February. The defenders knocked out seven tanks; the camp still fell, its last fight a matter of metres in which no bomb could help, and of about 500 CIDG some 160 were killed or missing and of 24 Americans eight were killed¹⁹. The base itself lived on air supply: about 14,356 tons came by air, 8,120 of them by parachute, and the air bridge cost a KC-130, three C-123s and 35 helicopters⁸³. Here the flak around the approaches, measured each night, was struck each night, and I estimate the air bridge lost about a third fewer helicopters²³.

The front command under Major General Tran Quy Hai pulled the 325C Division back from the hills in mid-March and the 304th from the eastern trenches before the end of the month, about two weeks before its historical withdrawal. Operation Pegasus, the 1st Cavalry Division's overland relief along Route 9, began on 1 April, linked up with the base on 8 April and ended on 15 April. Official US figures for the siege itself are more than 274 killed and 2,500 wounded; the PAVN acknowledged about 2,469 killed between January and July⁸³. I estimate that the garrison lost about a third fewer killed than the historical one, about 180, and that the PAVN's dead were about half again its acknowledged figure, perhaps 3,500–4,000 to July; both are judgments built on the historical figures and the change in what the bombs could reach, not counts. In this war Khe Sanh was the worst battle the PAVN fought in 1968, and the most complete American victory of the war on the ground. It was won at the place Hanoi had chosen, on the border, while the war was decided in the cities and in Washington; and the brigade taken from Thua Thien's hamlets to help win it was not in Thua Thien when the Front's local forces returned to them in February²³.

#### ◆ Delta at Tet: the command posts in Saigon and the files at Hue
*29 Jan – 8 Mar 1968 · Saigon (Districts 5, 6 and 8, the Phu Tho racetrack, Gia Dinh); Hue · new · EV014-6*
*Initiative: enemy*

In the ten days before Tet the unit raided eleven addresses in Gia Dinh and Saigon out of the task force's file and took two couriers of the city's sapper organisation, on 24 and 27 January. Neither knew anything about the timing of the offensive, and neither did the file. The national file named the Front's political apparatus, street by street in places, and the apparatus is not where the decision to attack at Tet was held or when it was transmitted; that is the finding the record had already made and this memorandum confirms from the other side of the same week³¹. The warning that reached the corps came from the task force's and MACV's reading of the troop movements, not from Delta's prisoners.

Once the attack came, the unit was used for what it was best at and not for what it was built for. Between 1 and 9 February three assault troops cleared fourteen Front command posts in Districts 5, 6 and 8 and around the Phu Tho racetrack, moving at night with goggles against men who had never fought anyone who could see in the dark; eight of the fourteen were taken without a shot fired at the entry. It was the best urban assault force in the theater and it was spent as infantry for nine days. It cost nine operators killed and thirty-one wounded, the unit's worst month of the war, about a tenth of the operators then in the country³¹.

At Hue a troop was flown in on 9 February on Vance's own authority with two Special Branch officers, to the Special Branch compound inside the triangle, to recover the province's and districts' infrastructure files before the Front's security service could finish with them. It recovered two of the seven district files and the province register's index. The rest had gone north with the Front's security cadres in the first week, as the record has it³¹. Two operators were killed in the compound's courtyard on 10 February. The judgment the memorandum takes from that morning is narrow and worth stating: a raid force can recover files a few days late and cannot prevent their capture, and the record's own account of what Hue cost the file-building effort stands unaltered.

#### The Tet Offensive: the first wave
*30–31 Jan 1968 · Saigon, Hue and the provincial and district capitals of South Vietnam · changed from history · EV000-42*
*Initiative: enemy*
Shortly after midnight on 30 January, a day early because of a confusion in calendars, VC and PAVN forces attacked Nha Trang, Ban Me Thuot, Kontum, Hoi An, Tuy Hoa, Da Nang, Qui Nhon and Pleiku in I and II Corps. The warning was not fully heeded: half the ARVN was on holiday leave for Tet. At 03:00 on 31 January the main wave struck Saigon, Cholon and Gia Dinh and the rest of the country. In all, between about 70,000 and 84,000 PAVN and VC troops attacked more than 100 towns and cities: 36 of the 44 provincial capitals, 5 of the 6 autonomous cities, between 64 and 72 of the 245 district towns, and the capital⁷⁹.

The plan called for the general offensive to set off a general uprising of the urban population. None came. In most towns the attackers were driven out within days by the ARVN, whose units, against American and Vietnamese expectations, generally fought, and by US forces; in Saigon, Hue, Ban Me Thuot, Kontum, Can Tho and Ben Tre the fighting lasted longer and destroyed large parts of the towns. The VC local forces and the urban cadres, who had surfaced to lead the uprising, were exposed and killed in large numbers. The United States claimed more than 45,000 PAVN and VC killed and 5,800 captured in the first phase, to 8 April, a figure even its own historians call excessively optimistic; Westmoreland gave 32,000 killed. Allied losses in the offensive were 2,788 ARVN killed, 8,299 wounded and 587 missing, and 1,536 US and other allied killed, 7,764 wounded and 11 missing. The PAVN official history gives communist casualties for the three phases of 1968 as 111,179, including 45,267 killed⁷⁹.

In this war the first wave strikes the same towns on the same night, with the same forces². The differences fall in IV and III Corps and in the district towns.

**The land and the police.** The battalions that attacked Can Tho and the Binh Thuy air base came up through the titled villages of Phong Dinh. GOLDEN SHEAF and the oil-spot of 1964–66 had left those villages in the hands of their own Popular Forces. PF platoons in at least six hamlets on the approaches reported the columns by AN/PRC-25 on the night of 30–31 January, and two of them fought. The 21st Division's reaction company and the Binh Thuy defenders were alerted about three hours before the assault².

The attackers also waited for guides from two villages on the Xa No canal. The guides never arrived, because both villages had been taken in the night check two weeks earlier³. The attackers reached the university and the market but not the corps headquarters. Can Tho was cleared in about four days, by the 21st Division's battalions and the RF of Phong Dinh with VNAF A-1H support, and far less of the town was destroyed than on the historical course. Vinh Long, where titling had run along the river since 1964, was cleared in about three days².

Ben Tre in Kien Hoa was the Front's oldest stronghold in the Delta. There the government had titled little outside the town, because it held little outside the town, and the battle and the destruction were as they were historically². The difference ran exactly along the line of night security that the land programme had followed since 1964.

In Can Tho, My Tho and Vinh Long the Special Branch's urban sections had files on about a third of the legal cadres who surfaced to lead the uprising. In the first two weeks of February they arrested about 1,700 of them, on those files and on the identifications of constables and ward chiefs³. The infrastructure driven from the villages had gone to the towns.

The PIOCCs at Can Tho, My Tho and Vinh Long had carried charts of the province committees since 1965. The charts held photographs from the biometric enrolment of the cadres' arrested relatives and ralliers. About 140 province- and district-committee cadres came into the three towns to form the "revolutionary committees". About 50 of them were taken by 15 February, most on the identifications of ward police who had their photographs, and the Can Tho committee never held its first meeting¹¹.

**The warning.** The checkpoint of memorandum 013 tested whether the files should have warned of Tet⁸². They held the pieces:
- the three Hoc Mon caches found in December from a courier's notebook;
- the province committees' preparations for the uprising in the Delta towns, which the district centres had been charting;
- the task force's national picture of the Front's political organisation.

What the files did not hold was the date or the scale. The order for the general offensive went down the military channels and reached the cadres in the towns only days before it, and the file saw the political half of the enemy, not the regiments⁸². The combined estimate of January 1968 read the pieces as preparations for attacks on the towns of the Delta and the Saigon belt around Tet, local in scale and secondary to Khe Sanh.

Two decisions above the level the files reached absorbed the warning, and they kept the national surprise:
- MACV judged the main effort to be in the north.
- The Joint General Staff's order that sent half the army on Tet leave stood until the first attacks in I and II Corps.

Below those decisions the warning showed. The III and IV Corps combined centres kept one reaction battalion in each divisional area at its towns over the holiday, and that is where the recall plans worked fastest⁸².

**The army in the towns.** By January 1968 about four in five ARVN battalion commanders had been chosen by the board and had held their battalions for more than a year. The board's commanders obeyed the leave order, but in IV and III Corps they had recall plans written to the career course's pattern¹².

The 21st Division's desertion rate had fallen since the census reached its home districts in 1966. It stood at about 90 percent of its authorised strength on the muster roll³. Of that, by the evening of 31 January, the battalions of the 7th, 9th and 21st Divisions had about 70 percent in the towns against about half historically, a little more where the centres had held a battalion back¹²⁸².

Every ARVN soldier and territorial had carried a modern carbine for four years, and had passed the ten-week or the six-week course. The difference is clearest in the district towns. There the first wave was made by VC local-force battalions and companies against garrisons of an RF company or two, a PF platoon and the district police. I estimate that the attackers penetrated about two-thirds of the 64–72 district towns attacked, against nearly all historically²¹. The ones they did not enter were held by RF companies firing from their compound walls at men crossing open ground under flare light, as at Long Huu in 1964. The companies fired as the territorial course taught, in pairs, with ammunition kept for the second rush, under lieutenants many of whom had been sergeants in the same companies two years before¹²¹⁵.

At Cai Lay on 31 January the 311th RF Company held the district compound until morning against a battalion of the 261st. It had one squad gun lent by the 7th Division and its own carbines. At 0600 it still had about a third of its basic load, and it was still firing at 0730, when the 2nd Battalion, 11th Regiment arrived. The battalion of the 261st had planned to be through the town by 0400²¹¹⁵.

In Can Tho, Captain Jerome Pike, adviser to the 1st Battalion, 31st Regiment, took the call of a PF corporal on the Xa No canal in Vietnamese at 2340 on 30 January. He moved the battalion's reaction company to the university before the attackers reached it. The 9th Division's 2nd Brigade and the Mobile Riverine Force had been together since 1967, and they fought the streets of My Tho and Vinh Long as the units they had trained as¹¹.

**The wounded.** Historically the ARVN's wounded in the offensive were about three times its dead⁷⁹. In this war more of those torn by a B40 fragment or a burst of AK-47 fire were alive at dawn, because the man beside them had put on a tourniquet. In Hue the wounded of the 1st Division and the Marines lay in the Citadel's streets for a month, and the course saved men every day of it. The surgeons at Phu Bai complained, as Nha Trang's had in 1965, about tourniquets left on too long¹⁵.

**The count.** Taking the rifles, the officers and the course together, I put the ARVN's killed in the first phase at about 2,150 against 2,788 historically²¹¹². The rifles account for about 290 of the difference, the officers for about 200 and the course for about 150. I put the American killed in the first phase at about 30–60 fewer, at the rate shown at the Ia Drang¹⁵. The warning in III and IV Corps does not move the count, because the dead fell at Saigon, Hue and the cities, where it changed nothing⁸². I do not adjust the enemy's losses, which were never measured to a precision these changes would show in.

None of this touched the outcome in Saigon, Hue or the Highlands, where the attackers were main-force units no district centre had charted³¹¹. At Hue the 1st Division under Truong, the list's first name since 1966, fought as it fought historically.

#### The posts of Cai Lay at Tet
*30 Jan – 2 Feb 1968 · Cai Lay and Cai Be Districts, Dinh Tuong Province; the post at Ap My Loi, My Phuoc Tay; the posts along the canal and Route 4 east to My Tho · new · EV010-4*
*Initiative: enemy*

The Front's battalions came through Cai Lay to attack My Tho, and most of the posts on the way were left alone. The 514th Local Force Battalion and the 261st and 263rd Main Force Battalions moved east toward the province capital on the nights of 29–31 January, along the canals north of Route 4, and the Dinh Tuong command's order was to isolate the posts on the route rather than take them, because the battalions needed to reach My Tho intact. Of about 34 PF posts in Cai Lay District, three were attacked and nine more were mortared to keep their platoons inside their wire. The post at Ap My Loi was not attacked at all. Its two-man listening post on the canal bank, kept out every night since 1964, reported at 2310 on 30 January a column of about 200 men with heavy weapons moving east on the far bank, and Sergeant Le Van Muoi sent the report up with the count and the time; it reached the 7th Division at Cai Lay at 0140, and with two other posts' reports it told the division that a battalion was going for My Tho, not for the district town. It changed nothing at My Tho, which the 261st, 263rd and 514th attacked at 0310 on 31 January and which was retaken in three days by the ARVN 7th Division and the US 9th Division's riverine battalions, as historically. It did keep the 247th RF Company off Route 4 that night, under Liem's rule of 1967⁶.

The post that fell was the one whose leader had held the platoon for three months. At Ap Hau Thanh, on the canal four kilometres east of Ap My Loi, the platoon's sergeant had been commissioned in the autumn of 1967 and the platoon was led by an acting leader, a corporal first class who had passed the leader's course and had not yet been given the platoon leader's course. He kept no listening post out on the night of 31 January, because the post had not been attacked in two years; a company of the 514th came over the wall at 0200 with satchel charges, and the post lost nine killed and its radio and weapons. Across the two districts in the three nights, the posts that kept their listening posts out were mortared and left; the one that did not was taken. The enemy chose which, and he chose by watching the routine⁶.

#### Tet without guides in Hoc Mon
*30 Jan – 4 Feb 1968 · Hoc Mon and Go Vap Districts, Gia Dinh Province; the approaches to Tan Son Nhut from Ba Queo and Vinh Loc · new · EV012-8*
*Initiative: enemy*

The battalions that attacked Saigon from the northwest at Tet came down the corridor the Hoc Mon committee had governed until 1965. They passed through Hoc Mon and Go Vap to the Ba Queo crossroads and the west side of Tan Son Nhut. Historically they were guided through it by the district's infrastructure, which had hidden weapons in the hamlets for months, quartered the assault troops in the market gardens on the night of the 29th and walked them to their start lines. In this war the Hoc Mon committee had governed from the Ho Bo woods since 1966, and its tax collectors had not lived in a Hoc Mon hamlet since. The Saigon–Gia Dinh Region gave the guiding of the northwestern columns to cadres sent in from Cu Chi in December 1967. They knew the roads and not the hamlets¹⁶.

Three things followed, and they were small. First, the weapons. Of the nine caches the region had placed in Hoc Mon's hamlets for the assault, the task force's III Corps task group had found three in December from a courier's notebook. It read them as the stocks of a local offensive against the district towns, not of a general offensive against the capital, and passed them to the combined estimate in those words. The warning did not change the surprise of 31 January, because nobody in Saigon read three caches in one district as the city's attack. But two companies of the northwestern columns reached their start lines short of their B-40 rockets¹⁶.

Second, the night of the 29th. Without resident cadres to quarter them, the assault companies spent the night of 29–30 January in the open fields west of Ba Queo instead of in houses. A PF platoon at Tan Thoi Nhi, the hamlet where STILL WATER had taken the security section in 1965, saw them at 2100 on the 30th and reported a company moving south. The report reached the district at 2140 and the combined III Corps centre at 2350. At 0100 the centre put the ARVN Airborne battalion standing by at Tan Son Nhut on half an hour's notice, two hours before the attack. That was not enough to change what happened at the west gate, and it is not a claim that it did¹⁶.

Third, the attack's timing. The northwestern columns, guided by men who did not know the hamlets' paths, reached Tan Son Nhut's western perimeter about one to two hours later than the region's plan, after the attacks on the city's centre had begun and the base was awake. The fight at the west gate and Ba Queo was the fight of the historical record, which the base's defenders and the 3rd Squadron, 4th Cavalry won by the afternoon of the 31st. The local cost to the attackers was higher. I put the dead of the two battalions at about a sixth above the historical count, on the evidence of the fields they crossed in daylight on the 31st¹⁶.

The event does not change Tet. It shows on one road what the attack on the infrastructure had done by 1968: the enemy could still assemble a battalion and send it to a gate, but no longer from inside the hamlets it passed through. A committee that governs from a wood eighteen kilometres away cannot hide a rocket in a market garden.

#### The battle for Saigon and the attack on the US embassy
*31 Jan – 7 Mar 1968 · Saigon, Cholon and Tan Son Nhut · historical · EV000-43*
At 02:45 on 31 January a 19-man team of the VC C-10 Sapper Battalion blew a hole in the wall of the new US embassy compound in Saigon and fought inside the grounds for more than six hours without entering the chancery; the compound was secured by 09:20, and five Americans were killed. The sappers were all killed or captured. Other VC units attacked Tan Son Nhut air base with three battalions, the Joint General Staff compound, the Presidential Palace and the naval headquarters, and held the national radio station for about six hours, though they could not broadcast⁷⁹. The image of the embassy under attack, broadcast in the United States hours after Westmoreland had declared the enemy's offensive capacity broken, did more political damage than any battle of the war.

The fighting in Saigon lasted weeks in Cholon, the Chinese quarter, where VC battalions held out around the Phu Tho racetrack. On 4 February residents were ordered out and the area declared a free-fire zone; the last battle in Cholon was fought on 7 March⁷⁹. On 1 February the national police chief, Brigadier General Nguyen Ngoc Loan, shot a captured VC officer in the head in a Saigon street before a photographer and a film camera. The image became, with the embassy, the American public's picture of Tet.

#### The battle of Hue
*31 Jan – 27 Feb 1968 · Hue, Thua Thien Province · changed from history · EV000-44*
*Initiative: enemy*
In the early hours of 31 January the PAVN 4th, 5th and 6th Regiments with the 1st, 2nd and 12th Sapper Battalions, under a Hue City Front command, took almost all of Hue, the old imperial capital, except the ARVN 1st Division headquarters in the northeast corner of the Citadel and the MACV advisory compound south of the Perfume River. Brigadier General Ngo Quang Truong held his headquarters with his staff and the Hac Bao Reconnaissance Company, and brought in the 3rd Regiment, the 4th Battalion, 2nd Regiment, the 1st Airborne Task Force (2nd and 7th Airborne Battalions), Vietnamese Marine Task Force A, the 7th Armored Cavalry Squadron and the 21st and 39th Ranger Battalions. The PAVN reinforced with the 7th Battalion, 29th Regiment of the 325C Division from Khe Sanh, the 803rd Regiment and a battalion of the 90th Regiment of the 324B Division, and the 6th Battalion, 24th Regiment of the 304th Division⁸⁴.

South of the river the 1st Battalion, 1st Marines and the 1st and 2nd Battalions, 5th Marines of Task Force X-Ray cleared the new city block by block, house by house, in rain and low cloud that restricted air support for most of the battle. Outside the city the 3rd Brigade of the 1st Cavalry Division, with the 2nd Battalion, 12th Cavalry, the 5th Battalion, 7th Cavalry and the 2nd Battalion, 501st Infantry, fought to cut the PAVN routes in from the west; historically it did not reach the Citadel's west wall until 25 February⁸⁴. Historically the ARVN and the Marines retook the Citadel, the flag of the Front came down from its tower on 24 February, and the battle was declared over on 2 March.

In this war Hue was one battle under one commander, and it ended about five days earlier. Historically it was three battles. Truong's division fought in the Citadel, the Marines fought south of the river under their own chain, and the 1st Cavalry Division's brigade fought to the west under the Army's, with no commander below Saigon over all three until MACV Forward was established at Phu Bai on 9 February. Here the I Corps combined centre at Da Nang, under Lieutenant General Hoang Xuan Lam with Lieutenant General Robert E. Cushman of III Marine Amphibious Force as his deputy, named Truong commander of the battle of Hue at 1100 on 1 February. Brigadier General Foster C. LaHue of Task Force X-Ray became his deputy for the south bank, and the 3rd Brigade's commander became his deputy for the western approaches. One fire-support coordination centre in the Citadel took every call for naval gunfire, Marine and Army artillery and the aircraft the weather allowed, from Vietnamese and American units alike, through the switchboard the combined command had run in I Corps since 1966¹⁶.

The change that shortened the battle was in the west. Truong's first request to the combined centre, on the afternoon of 1 February, was for the western routes to be cut before anything else, because the PAVN was reinforcing through La Chu and the hamlets of Thon Que Chu. It was the reinforcement, not the walls, that would make the Citadel long. The combined centre gave the 3rd Brigade first call on the I Corps helicopter lift from 2 February and placed the ARVN 1st Division's 3rd Regiment under the brigade's command for the block. Historically the brigade was short of lift and fought its way overland from PK-17. The brigade reached the Citadel's west wall on 20 February rather than 25 February. The 803rd Regiment's battalion from the 324B Division, which historically entered the city through La Chu in mid-February, was caught in the open at Thon La Chu on 12–13 February by the brigade and the ARVN 3rd Regiment together, with naval gunfire from the cruiser and destroyers of the gunfire support group off the coast, called through the Citadel's centre, and broken. The flag of the Front came down from the Citadel tower on 19 February, and the combined command declared the battle over on 27 February¹⁶. The weather and the walls still set most of the battle's length, and nothing in the combined command touched either.

The squad weapons made Hue somewhat less costly to retake and no shorter. On the first night the Hac Bao company held the 1st Division compound with M4A1s and four squad guns against the 800th and 802nd Battalions' assault teams. The difference was small, because the Hac Bao was among the few ARVN units historically re-armed by then. Across the river the Marines had two or three M249s in each squad and a marksman with an M110A2, who could put rounds into an upper-floor window at 200 metres from cover. The 2nd Battalion, 5th Marines credited its marksmen with most of the PAVN machine gunners killed on the treasury and the public health building between 4 and 6 February. What took the blocks was what took them historically: the 106mm recoilless rifles, the Ontos, tear gas and men going through walls. Against 458 ARVN and 250 American dead historically, the rifles alone would have made them about 410 and 215²¹. With the battle about five days shorter and the western reinforcement broken at La Chu, I put them at about 370 and 200, and the destruction a little less in the Citadel's western quarter, which was the last to be cleared¹⁶.

The ARVN lost 458 killed and about 2,700 wounded, and the Americans 250 killed and 1,554 wounded, on the historical course. PAVN and VC dead are given from 1,042 to more than 5,000, by source. Between 5,800 and 8,000 civilians died and the city was largely destroyed. During their occupation the PAVN and VC rounded up officials, officers, priests, teachers, foreigners and others on prepared lists; after the battle mass graves were found, and more than 2,000 people are known to have been executed, with the most cited count of bodies recovered at about 2,800⁸⁴. Hue was the only city the offensive held for more than a few days, and the killings there hardened the urban population of the South against the Front for the rest of the war. The five days the combined command saved were not days on which the security sections' killing stopped. Most of the executions were carried out in the first two weeks, and the shorter battle saved, I judge, a few hundred of the city's civilian dead at most, from the shelling of the western quarter rather than from the lists¹⁶.

In this war the battle is the same battle, and the killings are worse in one particular.
